Q: If two numbers are in the ratio 4:3. If 20 is added to both of the numbers then the ratio becomes 5:4 then find the smallest number?
Options:
A)60
B)30
C)40
D)20
E)50
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the smallest number in a pair of numbers that are in the ratio 4:3. When 20 is added to both numbers, the ratio changes to 5:4. To solve this, we will use algebra to represent the numbers and solve for the unknowns.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- The two numbers are in the ratio 4:3, so let the numbers be \( 4x \) and \( 3x \), where \( x \) is a positive constant.
- Adding 20 to both numbers changes the ratio to 5:4:
  \[
  \frac{4x + 20}{3x + 20} = \frac{5}{4}
  \]
- We need to solve for \( x \) and find the smallest number, which is \( 3x \).

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
**Method 1: Solve the ratio equation directly.**  
Set up the equation based on the given ratio and solve for \( x \). Use this value to compute \( 3x \).

**Method 2: Use trial and error with the options.**  
Substitute each option into the problem to check if it satisfies the conditions.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1: Solve the ratio equation directly**
1. Start with the equation:
   \[
   \frac{4x + 20}{3x + 20} = \frac{5}{4}
   \]
2. Cross-multiply to eliminate the fraction:
   \[
   4(4x + 20) = 5(3x + 20)
   \]
   Expand both sides:
   \[
   16x + 80 = 15x + 100
   \]
3. Simplify and solve for \( x \):
   \[
   16x - 15x = 100 - 80
   \]
   \[
   x = 20
   \]
4. Compute the smallest number:
   \[
   3x = 3(20) = 60
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use trial and error with the options**
1. Test each option to see if it satisfies the conditions:
   - **Option A (60):** If the smallest number is 60, then \( x = 20 \) (since \( 3x = 60 \)). The numbers are \( 4x = 80 \) and \( 3x = 60 \). Adding 20 to both gives \( 100 \) and \( 80 \), and the ratio is:
     \[
     \frac{100}{80} = \frac{5}{4}
     \]
     This satisfies the condition.
   - **Other options:** Testing other options (e.g., 30, 40, etc.) will not satisfy the ratio condition.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is systematic and guarantees the correct result.
- **Method 2** is less efficient but works well for multiple-choice problems.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Start with the equation:
   \[
   \frac{4x + 20}{3x + 20} = \frac{5}{4}
   \]
2. Cross-multiply:
   \[
   4(4x + 20) = 5(3x + 20)
   \]
   Expand:
   \[
   16x + 80 = 15x + 100
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = 20
   \]
4. Compute the smallest number:
   \[
   3x = 60
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Original numbers: \( 4x = 80 \), \( 3x = 60 \).
- After adding 20: \( 100 \) and \( 80 \).
- Ratio:
  \[
  \frac{100}{80} = \frac{5}{4}
  \]
  This confirms the solution.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **A) 60**.
Extracted Option:
A
Correct:
A

Q: A watch was sold at a loss of 10%. If the watch was sold for $32 more, there would have been a gain of 6%. What is the cost price?
Options:
A)$180
B)$200
C)$220
D)$240
E)$260
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the cost price of a watch given two scenarios: (1) it was sold at a loss of 10%, and (2) if sold for $32 more, there would have been a gain of 6%. This requires setting up equations based on the relationships between cost price, selling price, and profit/loss percentages.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Loss in the first scenario = 10% of cost price.
- Gain in the second scenario = 6% of cost price.
- Difference in selling prices between the two scenarios = $32.

Let the cost price of the watch be \( x \).  
Selling price in the first scenario = \( 0.9x \) (10% loss).  
Selling price in the second scenario = \( 1.06x \) (6% gain).  
Difference in selling prices: \( 1.06x - 0.9x = 32 \).

---

### Step 2: Solution methods:
#### Method 1: Solve using a single equation.
From the difference in selling prices:  
\[
1.06x - 0.9x = 32
\]
Simplify to find \( x \), the cost price.

#### Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.
The difference in selling prices corresponds to the combined percentage change (10% loss + 6% gain = 16% of cost price).  
Thus, \( 0.16x = 32 \). Solve for \( x \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:
#### Method 1:
\[
1.06x - 0.9x = 32
\]
\[
0.16x = 32
\]
Divide both sides by 0.16:
\[
x = \frac{32}{0.16} = 200
\]

#### Method 2:
The combined percentage change is 16% of the cost price:
\[
0.16x = 32
\]
Divide both sides by 0.16:
\[
x = \frac{32}{0.16} = 200
\]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods:
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 2 is more intuitive and avoids unnecessary steps. It directly relates the percentage change to the difference in selling prices.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:
Using Method 2:
\[
0.16x = 32
\]
\[
x = \frac{32}{0.16} = 200
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
If the cost price is \( x = 200 \):
- Selling price at 10% loss = \( 0.9 \times 200 = 180 \).
- Selling price at 6% gain = \( 1.06 \times 200 = 212 \).
Difference in selling prices = \( 212 - 180 = 32 \), which matches the problem statement.

---

### Final Answer:
The cost price of the watch is $200.  
The answer is **B**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: In what time will a train 100 meters long cross an electric pole, if its speed is 126 km/hr
Options:
A)8.5 seconds
B)2.86 seconds
C)3.5 seconds
D)2.5 seconds
E)2.6 seconds
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the time it takes for a train 100 meters long to cross an electric pole. The train's speed is given in km/hr, so we need to convert it to m/s for consistency with the length of the train. The time can then be calculated using the formula:  
\[
\text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}}
\]

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Length of the train (distance): \( 100 \, \text{m} \)
- Speed of the train: \( 126 \, \text{km/hr} \)
- Conversion factor: \( 1 \, \text{km/hr} = \frac{5}{18} \, \text{m/s} \)
- The train crosses the pole completely, so the distance covered is equal to the length of the train.

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Convert the speed from km/hr to m/s.  
2. Use the formula \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} \) to calculate the time.

**Method 2:**  
1. Use the formula \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} \) directly, but first express the speed in terms of km/hr and convert it to m/s during the calculation.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1:**
1. Convert speed:  
   \[
   126 \, \text{km/hr} = 126 \times \frac{5}{18} \, \text{m/s} = 35 \, \text{m/s}
   \]
2. Calculate time:  
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} = \frac{100}{35} \, \text{s} = 2.86 \, \text{s}
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. Use the formula \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} \), but convert speed during the calculation:  
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{100}{126 \times \frac{5}{18}} = \frac{100}{35} = 2.86 \, \text{s}
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result (\( 2.86 \, \text{s} \)). However, **Method 1** is more straightforward because it separates the conversion step from the calculation, making it easier to follow.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
Using **Method 1**:
1. Convert speed:  
   \[
   126 \, \text{km/hr} = 35 \, \text{m/s}
   \]
2. Calculate time:  
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{100}{35} = 2.86 \, \text{s}
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
1. Reverse the calculation:  
   \[
   \text{Speed} \times \text{Time} = 35 \, \text{m/s} \times 2.86 \, \text{s} = 100 \, \text{m}
   \]
   This confirms the calculation is correct.

2. Verify units:  
   - Distance: \( \text{m} \)  
   - Speed: \( \text{m/s} \)  
   - Time: \( \text{s} \)  
   All units are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The time it takes for the train to cross the electric pole is **2.86 seconds**.  
The answer is **B**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: A, B and C rent a pasture. If A puts 10 oxen for 7 months, B puts 12 oxen for 5 months and C puts 15 oxen for 3 months for grazing and the rent of the pasture is Rs. 175, then how much amount should C pay as his share of rent?
Options:
A)40
B)45
C)49
D)51
E)43
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how much rent C should pay for the pasture. The rent is shared based on the product of the number of oxen and the number of months each person uses the pasture. This requires calculating the proportional contribution of each person to the total usage and then distributing the total rent accordingly.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- A uses **10 oxen for 7 months**.
- B uses **12 oxen for 5 months**.
- C uses **15 oxen for 3 months**.
- Total rent = Rs. 175.
- Rent is proportional to the product of oxen and months (i.e., "oxen-months").

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct proportional calculation
1. Compute the total "oxen-months" for A, B, and C.
2. Find the total "oxen-months" for all three combined.
3. Calculate the proportion of C's "oxen-months" to the total.
4. Multiply this proportion by the total rent to find C's share.

#### Method 2: Simplify using ratios
1. Express the "oxen-months" for A, B, and C as a ratio.
2. Use the ratio to divide the total rent directly, assigning C's share.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1: Direct proportional calculation
1. Compute "oxen-months" for each:
   - A: \( 10 \times 7 = 70 \)
   - B: \( 12 \times 5 = 60 \)
   - C: \( 15 \times 3 = 45 \)
2. Total "oxen-months":  
   \[
   70 + 60 + 45 = 175
   \]
3. Proportion of C's "oxen-months" to total:  
   \[
   \frac{C's \, oxen\text{-}months}{Total \, oxen\text{-}months} = \frac{45}{175}
   \]
4. C's share of rent:  
   \[
   \text{C's share} = \frac{45}{175} \times 175 = Rs. 45
   \]

#### Method 2: Simplify using ratios
1. Express "oxen-months" as a ratio:  
   \[
   A : B : C = 70 : 60 : 45
   \]
2. Simplify the ratio:  
   \[
   A : B : C = 14 : 12 : 9
   \]
3. Total parts in the ratio:  
   \[
   14 + 12 + 9 = 35
   \]
4. C's share of rent:  
   \[
   \text{C's share} = \frac{C's \, parts}{Total \, parts} \times \text{Total rent} = \frac{9}{35} \times 175 = Rs. 45
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods:
Both methods yield the same result. Method 2 is slightly more efficient because it avoids intermediate proportional calculations by working directly with ratios.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 2):
1. Ratio of "oxen-months": \( 14 : 12 : 9 \).
2. Total parts = \( 35 \).
3. C's share:  
   \[
   \frac{9}{35} \times 175 = Rs. 45
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
1. Total rent distribution:  
   - A's share: \( \frac{14}{35} \times 175 = Rs. 70 \),
   - B's share: \( \frac{12}{35} \times 175 = Rs. 60 \),
   - C's share: \( \frac{9}{35} \times 175 = Rs. 45 \).
2. Verify total:  
   \[
   70 + 60 + 45 = 175
   \]
   ✅ Consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **B**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: Find the numbers which are in the ratio 3:2:4 such that the sum of the first and the second added to the difference of the third and the second is 21.
Options:
A)9, 6, 16
B)9, 6, 19
C)9, 6, 16
D)9, 6, 12
E)9, 6, 11
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding three numbers in the ratio 3:2:4 such that the sum of the first and second numbers, added to the difference between the third and second numbers, equals 21. The strategy involves expressing the numbers in terms of a common variable, solving for that variable, and verifying the condition.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- The numbers are in the ratio 3:2:4, so let the numbers be \( 3x \), \( 2x \), and \( 4x \), where \( x \) is a positive constant.
- The condition is: \( (3x + 2x) + (4x - 2x) = 21 \).

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
**Method 1:** Solve the equation directly by substituting the expressions for the numbers into the condition and solving for \( x \). Then calculate the actual numbers.

**Method 2:** Use trial and error with the given options, checking which set of numbers satisfies both the ratio and the condition.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1: Solve algebraically**
1. Substitute the expressions for the numbers into the condition:
   \[
   (3x + 2x) + (4x - 2x) = 21
   \]
2. Simplify:
   \[
   5x + 2x = 21
   \]
   \[
   7x = 21
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = 3
   \]
4. Calculate the numbers:
   \[
   3x = 3(3) = 9, \quad 2x = 2(3) = 6, \quad 4x = 4(3) = 12
   \]

#### **Method 2: Trial and error**
1. Check each option to see if the numbers satisfy the ratio 3:2:4 and the condition \( (a + b) + (c - b) = 21 \):
   - **Option A (9, 6, 16):**
     \[
     (9 + 6) + (16 - 6) = 15 + 10 = 25 \quad \text{(does not work)}.
     \]
   - **Option B (9, 6, 19):**
     \[
     (9 + 6) + (19 - 6) = 15 + 13 = 28 \quad \text{(does not work)}.
     \]
   - **Option C (9, 6, 16):**
     \[
     (9 + 6) + (16 - 6) = 15 + 10 = 25 \quad \text{(does not work)}.
     \]
   - **Option D (9, 6, 12):**
     \[
     (9 + 6) + (12 - 6) = 15 + 6 = 21 \quad \text{(works)}.
     \]
   - **Option E (9, 6, 11):**
     \[
     (9 + 6) + (11 - 6) = 15 + 5 = 20 \quad \text{(does not work)}.
     \]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
- **Method 1** is systematic and guarantees the correct result by solving the equation directly.
- **Method 2** is less efficient, as it requires testing all options, but it serves as a good verification method.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Solve \( 7x = 21 \) to get \( x = 3 \).
2. Calculate the numbers:
   \[
   3x = 9, \quad 2x = 6, \quad 4x = 12
   \]
3. Verify the condition:
   \[
   (9 + 6) + (12 - 6) = 15 + 6 = 21 \quad \text{(correct)}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check
- The numbers \( 9, 6, 12 \) are in the ratio 3:2:4.
- The condition \( (9 + 6) + (12 - 6) = 21 \) is satisfied.

---

The answer is **D**.
Extracted Option:
D
Correct:
D

Q: Find smallest prime number is:
Options:
A)6
B)18
C)7
D)15
E)11
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to identify the smallest prime number from the given options. A prime number is defined as a number greater than 1 that has no divisors other than 1 and itself. The strategy involves checking each option to determine if it is prime and then selecting the smallest one.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- A prime number has exactly two distinct positive divisors: 1 and itself.
- The options provided are:  
  A) 6, B) 18, C) 7, D) 15, E) 11.

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
Check each option individually to determine if it is prime by testing divisibility. Then identify the smallest prime number.

**Method 2:**  
List all prime numbers less than or equal to the largest option (18) and compare them to the given options. Select the smallest one.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1: Check each option**
- **Option A: 6**  
  Divisors of 6 are 1, 2, 3, and 6. Since it has more than two divisors, it is not prime.

- **Option B: 18**  
  Divisors of 18 are 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, and 18. Since it has more than two divisors, it is not prime.

- **Option C: 7**  
  Divisors of 7 are 1 and 7. Since it has exactly two divisors, it is prime.

- **Option D: 15**  
  Divisors of 15 are 1, 3, 5, and 15. Since it has more than two divisors, it is not prime.

- **Option E: 11**  
  Divisors of 11 are 1 and 11. Since it has exactly two divisors, it is prime.

From this method, the prime numbers are 7 and 11. The smallest is **7**.

---

#### **Method 2: List all primes ≤ 18**
The prime numbers less than or equal to 18 are:  
2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17.  

From the given options, the primes are 7 (Option C) and 11 (Option E). The smallest is **7**.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result: the smallest prime number is 7. Method 1 is more direct for this problem, as it involves checking only the given options.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
- Check each option for primality:
  - 6: Not prime.
  - 18: Not prime.
  - 7: Prime.
  - 15: Not prime.
  - 11: Prime.
- The smallest prime is **7**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check
- Verify primality of 7: Divisors are 1 and 7 → Prime.
- Verify primality of 11: Divisors are 1 and 11 → Prime.
- Compare: 7 < 11 → Smallest is 7.

---

The answer is **C**.
Extracted Option:
C
Correct:
C

Q: How many integers Q are prime numbers in the range 200 < Q < 220?
Options:
A) 1
B) 2
C) 3
D) 4
E) 5
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding how many prime numbers exist in the range \( 200 < Q < 220 \). A prime number is a positive integer greater than 1 that has no divisors other than 1 and itself. The strategy involves identifying all integers in the range and testing each for primality.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- The range is \( 200 < Q < 220 \), meaning \( Q \) can be \( 201, 202, \dots, 219 \).
- A prime number has no divisors other than 1 and itself.
- We will test each number in the range for primality.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1: Direct divisibility testing**  
- For each number \( Q \) in the range, check divisibility by all integers from 2 to \( \sqrt{Q} \). If no divisors are found, \( Q \) is prime.

**Method 2: Use a prime sieve or known prime list**  
- Use a list of known primes or a sieve algorithm to identify primes in the range \( 200 < Q < 220 \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1: Direct divisibility testing**
1. List all integers in the range: \( 201, 202, \dots, 219 \).
2. Test each number for primality:
   - \( 201 \): Divisible by 3 → Not prime.
   - \( 202 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 203 \): Divisible by 7 → Not prime.
   - \( 204 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 205 \): Divisible by 5 → Not prime.
   - \( 206 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 207 \): Divisible by 3 → Not prime.
   - \( 208 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 209 \): Divisible by 11 → Not prime.
   - \( 210 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 211 \): No divisors from 2 to \( \sqrt{211} \) → Prime.
   - \( 212 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 213 \): Divisible by 3 → Not prime.
   - \( 214 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 215 \): Divisible by 5 → Not prime.
   - \( 216 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 217 \): Divisible by 7 → Not prime.
   - \( 218 \): Divisible by 2 → Not prime.
   - \( 219 \): Divisible by 3 → Not prime.
3. Prime numbers: \( 211 \).

#### **Method 2: Use a prime sieve or known prime list**
1. Use a list of primes or a sieve algorithm to identify primes between 200 and 220.
2. Known primes in this range: \( 211 \).
3. Prime numbers: \( 211 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is thorough and directly tests each number for primality, but it is computationally intensive for larger ranges.
- **Method 2** is faster if a prime list or sieve is available, but it relies on external resources.
- For this problem, both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferred for manual calculations.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
Using **Method 1**, the only prime number in the range \( 200 < Q < 220 \) is \( 211 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Verify \( 211 \) is prime: No divisors from 2 to \( \sqrt{211} \).
- Confirm no other primes exist in the range.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **A**.
Extracted Option:
A
Correct:

Q: If the average (arithmetic mean) of five consecutive negative integers is 2k – 1, what is the difference V between the greatest and least of the five integers?
Options:
A)4K
B)4k^2 -4k
C)4-4k
D)4
E)4k+4
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the difference \( V \) between the greatest and least of five consecutive negative integers, given that their average is \( 2k - 1 \). The integers are consecutive, so their difference is fixed and does not depend on \( k \). The strategy involves expressing the integers in terms of their average and calculating the difference between the largest and smallest.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
1. The integers are consecutive and negative: Let them be \( x-2, x-1, x, x+1, x+2 \), where \( x \) is the middle integer.
2. The average of these integers is \( 2k - 1 \), so:
   \[
   \frac{(x-2) + (x-1) + x + (x+1) + (x+2)}{5} = 2k - 1
   \]
3. The difference \( V \) between the greatest and least integers is:
   \[
   V = (x+2) - (x-2) = 4
   \]

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
#### Method 1: Solve for \( x \) explicitly
- Use the average formula to solve for \( x \) in terms of \( k \), then calculate \( V \) directly.

#### Method 2: Use properties of consecutive integers
- Recognize that for any five consecutive integers, the difference between the greatest and least is always 4, regardless of the average.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods
#### Method 1: Solve for \( x \)
1. The sum of the integers is:
   \[
   (x-2) + (x-1) + x + (x+1) + (x+2) = 5x
   \]
2. The average is given as \( 2k - 1 \), so:
   \[
   \frac{5x}{5} = 2k - 1 \implies x = 2k - 1
   \]
3. The integers are \( (2k-3), (2k-2), (2k-1), (2k), (2k+1) \).
4. The greatest integer is \( 2k+1 \), and the least is \( 2k-3 \). Their difference is:
   \[
   V = (2k+1) - (2k-3) = 4
   \]

#### Method 2: Use properties of consecutive integers
1. For any five consecutive integers \( x-2, x-1, x, x+1, x+2 \), the difference between the greatest and least is:
   \[
   V = (x+2) - (x-2) = 4
   \]
2. The value of \( k \) does not affect \( V \), as the difference is fixed for any set of five consecutive integers.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** explicitly solves for \( x \) and confirms the result, but it is more algebraically intensive.
- **Method 2** leverages the fixed property of consecutive integers, making it simpler and more efficient.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 2)
1. The difference between the greatest and least of five consecutive integers is always:
   \[
   V = 4
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
1. Using Method 1, we calculated \( V = 4 \) explicitly.
2. Using Method 2, we confirmed that \( V = 4 \) is a fixed property of consecutive integers.
3. Both methods agree, so the result is consistent and accurate.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **D**.
Extracted Option:
D
Correct:
D

Q: A room contains 140 people, 15 percent of whom are women. A group of people, 30 percent of whom are women, leaves the room. Of the people remaining in the room, 10 percent are women. How many people left the room?
Options:
A)10
B)35
C)40
D)60
E)80
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many people left the room. The problem involves percentages of women in the room before and after a group leaves. We need to calculate the number of people in the group that left based on the given percentages and the total number of people in the room.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total people in the room initially: **140**
- Percentage of women initially: **15%** → Number of women initially = \( 0.15 \times 140 = 21 \)
- A group leaves the room, **30% of whom are women**.
- After the group leaves, **10% of the remaining people are women**.
- Goal: Determine the number of people in the group that left.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
#### **Method 1: Use algebra to calculate the group size**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of people who leave the room.
2. After \( x \) people leave, the remaining number of people is \( 140 - x \).
3. The number of women in the group that left is \( 0.3x \), and the number of men in the group that left is \( 0.7x \).
4. After the group leaves, the number of women remaining is \( 21 - 0.3x \), and the total number of people remaining is \( 140 - x \).
5. According to the problem, **10% of the remaining people are women**:
   \[
   \frac{21 - 0.3x}{140 - x} = 0.1
   \]

#### **Method 2: Trial and error with the given options**
1. Use the options to test possible values for \( x \) (the number of people who leave).
2. For each option, calculate the number of women and men who leave, the number of people remaining, and verify if 10% of the remaining people are women.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods
#### **Method 1: Algebraic solution**
1. Start with the equation:
   \[
   \frac{21 - 0.3x}{140 - x} = 0.1
   \]
2. Multiply through by \( 140 - x \) to eliminate the denominator:
   \[
   21 - 0.3x = 0.1(140 - x)
   \]
3. Expand and simplify:
   \[
   21 - 0.3x = 14 - 0.1x
   \]
   \[
   21 - 14 = 0.3x - 0.1x
   \]
   \[
   7 = 0.2x
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = \frac{7}{0.2} = 35
   \]

#### **Method 2: Trial and error**
1. Test \( x = 35 \):
   - Number of people remaining: \( 140 - 35 = 105 \)
   - Number of women who leave: \( 0.3 \times 35 = 10.5 \)
   - Number of women remaining: \( 21 - 10.5 = 10.5 \)
   - Percentage of women remaining: \( \frac{10.5}{105} = 0.1 = 10\% \) ✅
2. Test other options (e.g., \( x = 10, 40, 60, 80 \)):
   - None satisfy the condition that 10% of the remaining people are women.

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
- **Method 1** is systematic and guarantees the correct result through algebra.
- **Method 2** is faster if the correct option is tested early but requires more effort if the correct option is not obvious.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
Using the equation:
\[
\frac{21 - 0.3x}{140 - x} = 0.1
\]
We solved for \( x = 35 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check
1. Number of people who leave: \( x = 35 \).
2. Number of people remaining: \( 140 - 35 = 105 \).
3. Number of women who leave: \( 0.3 \times 35 = 10.5 \).
4. Number of women remaining: \( 21 - 10.5 = 10.5 \).
5. Percentage of women remaining: \( \frac{10.5}{105} = 0.1 = 10\% \). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **B) 35**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: The average age of a husband and a wife is 23 years when they were married five years ago but now the average age of the husband, wife and child is 22 years(the child was born during the interval). What is the present age of the child?
Options:
A)10 years
B)20 years
C)30 years
D)40 years
E)25 years
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the present age of the child. The problem provides the average age of the husband and wife at two different points in time: five years ago and now (including the child). Using these averages, we can calculate the ages of the husband, wife, and child.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
1. Five years ago, the average age of the husband and wife was 23 years.
   - Total age of husband and wife five years ago = \( 23 \times 2 = 46 \) years.
   - Present total age of husband and wife = \( 46 + 5 + 5 = 56 \) years.

2. Now, the average age of the husband, wife, and child is 22 years.
   - Total age of husband, wife, and child now = \( 22 \times 3 = 66 \) years.

3. The child was born during the interval, so the child’s age is included in the total age now.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction of total ages
- Subtract the present total age of the husband and wife from the total age of the husband, wife, and child to find the child’s age.

#### Method 2: Use algebraic equations
- Let the child’s present age be \( x \). Write an equation for the total age of the husband, wife, and child, and solve for \( x \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct subtraction
1. Present total age of husband and wife = \( 56 \) years.
2. Present total age of husband, wife, and child = \( 66 \) years.
3. Child’s age = \( 66 - 56 = 10 \) years.

---

#### Method 2: Algebraic equations
1. Let the child’s present age be \( x \).
2. Present total age of husband, wife, and child = \( 56 + x \).
3. Given that the average age of the husband, wife, and child is 22 years:
   \[
   \frac{56 + x}{3} = 22
   \]
4. Multiply through by 3:
   \[
   56 + x = 66
   \]
5. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = 66 - 56 = 10
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is simpler and more direct. Method 2 involves setting up an equation, which is useful for more complex problems but unnecessary here.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
Using Method 1:
- Present total age of husband and wife = \( 56 \) years.
- Present total age of husband, wife, and child = \( 66 \) years.
- Child’s age = \( 66 - 56 = 10 \) years.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
1. Five years ago, total age of husband and wife = \( 46 \) years.
2. Present total age of husband and wife = \( 46 + 10 = 56 \) years.
3. Present total age of husband, wife, and child = \( 56 + 10 = 66 \) years.
4. Average age of husband, wife, and child = \( \frac{66}{3} = 22 \), which matches the problem statement.

---

### Final Answer:
The present age of the child is \( 10 \) years.  
The answer is **A**.
Extracted Option:
A
Correct:
A

Q: A batsman scored 120 runs whichincluded 3 boundaries and 8sixes. What % of his total score did he make by running between the wickets
Options:
A)30 %
B)50 %
C)60 %
D)70 %
E)80 %
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the percentage of the batsman's total score that he made by running between the wickets. To do this, we need to calculate the runs scored from boundaries (4s and 6s), subtract this from the total score to find the runs made by running, and then compute the percentage of the total score that these runs represent.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total score = 120 runs
- Number of boundaries (4s) = 3
- Number of sixes (6s) = 8
- Runs from boundaries = \( 3 \times 4 = 12 \)
- Runs from sixes = \( 8 \times 6 = 48 \)
- Total runs from boundaries and sixes = \( 12 + 48 = 60 \)
- Runs made by running = \( 120 - 60 = 60 \)

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the runs made by running as \( \text{Total score} - \text{Runs from boundaries and sixes} \).  
2. Compute the percentage of the total score made by running using the formula:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage} = \left( \frac{\text{Runs made by running}}{\text{Total score}} \right) \times 100
   \]

**Method 2:**  
1. Directly calculate the percentage of runs scored from boundaries and sixes as:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage from boundaries and sixes} = \left( \frac{\text{Runs from boundaries and sixes}}{\text{Total score}} \right) \times 100
   \]  
2. Subtract this percentage from 100% to find the percentage of runs made by running.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1:**
1. Runs made by running = \( 120 - 60 = 60 \).  
2. Percentage of runs made by running:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage} = \left( \frac{60}{120} \right) \times 100 = 50\%
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. Percentage of runs from boundaries and sixes:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage from boundaries and sixes} = \left( \frac{60}{120} \right) \times 100 = 50\%
   \]  
2. Percentage of runs made by running:  
   \[
   100\% - 50\% = 50\%
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more direct and intuitive because it focuses on the runs made by running from the start, avoiding unnecessary intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Runs made by running = \( 120 - 60 = 60 \).  
2. Percentage of runs made by running:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage} = \left( \frac{60}{120} \right) \times 100 = 50\%
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Total runs = 120.  
- Runs from boundaries and sixes = \( 12 + 48 = 60 \).  
- Runs made by running = \( 120 - 60 = 60 \).  
- Percentage of runs made by running:  
  \[
  \left( \frac{60}{120} \right) \times 100 = 50\%
  \]  
The calculations are consistent and accurate.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **B**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: The tax on a commodity is diminished by 10% and its consumption increases by 10%. Find the effects on revenue ?
Options:
A)1% decrease
B)2% increase
C)3% decrease
D)4% decrease
E)none of these
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
The problem involves determining the effect on revenue when the tax on a commodity is reduced by 10% and its consumption increases by 10%. Revenue is proportional to the product of tax rate and consumption. We need to calculate the net percentage change in revenue.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Let the initial tax rate be \( T \) and the initial consumption be \( C \).
- Initial revenue is \( R_1 = T \times C \).
- After the tax reduction, the new tax rate becomes \( T' = 0.9T \) (10% reduction).
- After the consumption increase, the new consumption becomes \( C' = 1.1C \) (10% increase).
- New revenue is \( R_2 = T' \times C' = (0.9T) \times (1.1C) \).
- We need to calculate the percentage change in revenue:  
  \[
  \text{Percentage change} = \frac{R_2 - R_1}{R_1} \times 100
  \]

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using algebraic expressions.
- Compute \( R_2 \) in terms of \( R_1 \) and find the percentage change.

#### Method 2: Use a numerical example.
- Assume specific values for \( T \) and \( C \) (e.g., \( T = 100 \), \( C = 100 \)) to compute \( R_1 \) and \( R_2 \), then calculate the percentage change.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation**
1. Initial revenue:  
   \[
   R_1 = T \times C
   \]
2. New revenue:  
   \[
   R_2 = T' \times C' = (0.9T) \times (1.1C) = 0.99(T \times C)
   \]
3. Percentage change:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage change} = \frac{R_2 - R_1}{R_1} \times 100 = \frac{0.99R_1 - R_1}{R_1} \times 100 = \frac{-0.01R_1}{R_1} \times 100 = -1\%
   \]

#### **Method 2: Numerical example**
1. Assume \( T = 100 \) and \( C = 100 \).  
   Initial revenue:  
   \[
   R_1 = T \times C = 100 \times 100 = 10,000
   \]
2. New tax rate: \( T' = 0.9T = 0.9 \times 100 = 90 \).  
   New consumption: \( C' = 1.1C = 1.1 \times 100 = 110 \).  
   New revenue:  
   \[
   R_2 = T' \times C' = 90 \times 110 = 9,900
   \]
3. Percentage change:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage change} = \frac{R_2 - R_1}{R_1} \times 100 = \frac{9,900 - 10,000}{10,000} \times 100 = \frac{-100}{10,000} \times 100 = -1\%
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one.
Both methods yield the same result: a 1% decrease in revenue. Method 1 is preferred because it uses general algebraic reasoning and avoids reliance on specific numerical values.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method.
Using Method 1:
- New revenue: \( R_2 = 0.99R_1 \).
- Percentage change: \( \text{Percentage change} = -1\% \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy.
- Reverse calculation: If revenue decreases by 1%, the new revenue is \( 99\% \) of the original revenue, which matches \( R_2 = 0.99R_1 \).  
- Both methods confirm the result.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **A) 1% decrease**.
Extracted Option:
A
Correct:
A

Q: On dividing a number by 357, we get 37 as remainder.On dividing the same number by 17, what will be the remainder ?
Options:
A)4
B)3
C)8
D)7
E)2
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the remainder when a number \( N \), which leaves a remainder of 37 when divided by 357, is divided by 17. The problem involves modular arithmetic, and we can use the properties of remainders to solve it. The strategy involves expressing \( N \) in terms of its remainder modulo 357 and then reducing it modulo 17.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- \( N \) leaves a remainder of 37 when divided by 357. This means:
  \[
  N = 357k + 37 \quad \text{for some integer } k.
  \]
- We need to find the remainder when \( N \) is divided by 17.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
#### **Method 1: Direct modular reduction**
1. Since \( N = 357k + 37 \), we focus on the remainder of \( 37 \) modulo \( 17 \), as \( 357k \) is a multiple of \( 357 \) and does not affect the remainder modulo \( 17 \).
2. Compute \( 37 \mod 17 \) directly.

#### **Method 2: Modular arithmetic with \( 357 \)**
1. Break \( 357 \) into its remainder modulo \( 17 \), as \( 357 \equiv 0 \mod 357 \) but not modulo \( 17 \).
2. Use the equivalence \( N \equiv 37 \mod 357 \) and reduce \( 37 \mod 17 \) step-by-step.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods
#### **Method 1: Direct modular reduction**
1. Compute \( 37 \mod 17 \):
   \[
   37 \div 17 = 2 \quad \text{remainder } 3.
   \]
   Thus, \( 37 \equiv 3 \mod 17 \).

#### **Method 2: Modular arithmetic with \( 357 \)**
1. First, reduce \( 357 \mod 17 \):
   \[
   357 \div 17 = 21 \quad \text{remainder } 0.
   \]
   So, \( 357 \equiv 0 \mod 17 \).
2. Since \( N = 357k + 37 \), we have:
   \[
   N \equiv 37 \mod 17.
   \]
3. Compute \( 37 \mod 17 \) as in Method 1:
   \[
   37 \div 17 = 2 \quad \text{remainder } 3.
   \]
   Thus, \( N \equiv 3 \mod 17 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result (\( 3 \)), but Method 1 is more direct and efficient. Method 2 involves additional steps that are unnecessary for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
Using Method 1:
\[
37 \mod 17 = 3.
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
1. Verify by reconstructing \( N \):
   - \( N = 357k + 37 \), so \( N \mod 17 = (357k \mod 17) + (37 \mod 17) \).
   - Since \( 357 \equiv 0 \mod 17 \), we have \( N \mod 17 = 37 \mod 17 = 3 \).
2. The calculations are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **B) 3**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: There are 10 teams in a certain league and each team plays each of the other teams exactly twice. If each game is played by 2 teams, what is the total number of games played?
Options:
A)15
B)16
C)28
D)56
E)90
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the total number of games played in a league with 10 teams, where each team plays every other team exactly twice. This involves calculating the number of unique pairings of teams and then multiplying by 2 (since each pairing results in two games).

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total number of teams = 10
- Each team plays every other team twice.
- A game involves 2 teams.
- The number of unique pairings of teams can be calculated using combinations: \( \binom{n}{2} = \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \), where \( n \) is the total number of teams.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Use combinations directly.
- Calculate the number of unique pairings of teams using \( \binom{10}{2} = \frac{10 \cdot 9}{2} \).
- Multiply the result by 2 to account for the fact that each pairing results in two games.

#### Method 2: Use a systematic counting approach.
- Each team plays against 9 other teams (since there are 10 teams total).
- Multiply the number of games per team by the total number of teams, then divide by 2 (to avoid double-counting games between pairs of teams). Multiply the result by 2 to account for the fact that each pairing results in two games.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:
#### Method 1:
1. Calculate the number of unique pairings:
   \[
   \binom{10}{2} = \frac{10 \cdot 9}{2} = 45
   \]
2. Multiply by 2 (since each pairing results in two games):
   \[
   45 \cdot 2 = 90
   \]

#### Method 2:
1. Each team plays against 9 other teams, resulting in \( 10 \cdot 9 = 90 \) total games if we count each game twice (once for each team).
2. Divide by 2 to avoid double-counting:
   \[
   \frac{90}{2} = 45
   \]
3. Multiply by 2 (since each pairing results in two games):
   \[
   45 \cdot 2 = 90
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is more direct and uses the standard formula for combinations, making it easier to generalize to similar problems. Method 2 involves more steps and is less intuitive.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
Using Method 1:
1. Calculate the number of unique pairings:
   \[
   \binom{10}{2} = \frac{10 \cdot 9}{2} = 45
   \]
2. Multiply by 2:
   \[
   45 \cdot 2 = 90
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify using Method 2:
   \[
   \text{Total games} = \frac{10 \cdot 9}{2} \cdot 2 = 90
   \]
- Both methods confirm the result.

---

### Final Answer:
The total number of games played is 90.  
The answer is **E**.

Q: A clock shows the time as 3:30 P.m. If the minute hand gains 2 minutes every hour, how many minutes will the clock gain by 2 a.m.?
Options:
A)23 Minutes
B)21 Minutes
C)25 Minutes
D)26 Minutes
E)None of these
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to calculate how many minutes the clock's minute hand will gain by 2 a.m. if it gains 2 minutes every hour. We need to determine the total number of hours between 3:30 p.m. and 2 a.m., then multiply this by the rate of gain (2 minutes per hour).

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Start time: 3:30 p.m.
- End time: 2:00 a.m.
- Rate of gain: 2 minutes per hour.
- Total time interval: From 3:30 p.m. to 2:00 a.m.
- Assumption: The clock consistently gains 2 minutes every hour.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total time interval in hours and fractions of an hour.  
2. Multiply the total time by the rate of gain (2 minutes per hour).

**Method 2:**  
1. Break the time interval into two parts: from 3:30 p.m. to midnight, and from midnight to 2:00 a.m.  
2. Calculate the number of hours in each part, sum them, and multiply by the rate of gain.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1: Total time interval in hours**
1. From 3:30 p.m. to 2:00 a.m. is a total of **10.5 hours** (3:30 p.m. to midnight is 8.5 hours, and midnight to 2:00 a.m. is 2 hours).  
2. Multiply the total time by the rate of gain:  
   \[
   10.5 \, \text{hours} \times 2 \, \text{minutes/hour} = 21 \, \text{minutes}.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Break into two parts**
1. From 3:30 p.m. to midnight:  
   - This is **8.5 hours**.  
   - Gain = \( 8.5 \, \text{hours} \times 2 \, \text{minutes/hour} = 17 \, \text{minutes} \).  
2. From midnight to 2:00 a.m.:  
   - This is **2 hours**.  
   - Gain = \( 2 \, \text{hours} \times 2 \, \text{minutes/hour} = 4 \, \text{minutes} \).  
3. Total gain:  
   \[
   17 \, \text{minutes} + 4 \, \text{minutes} = 21 \, \text{minutes}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result of 21 minutes. Method 1 is more direct, while Method 2 provides a detailed breakdown. Both are valid, but Method 1 is preferred for its simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Total time interval = 10.5 hours.  
2. Gain = \( 10.5 \, \text{hours} \times 2 \, \text{minutes/hour} = 21 \, \text{minutes} \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check
1. Break the time into parts:  
   - From 3:30 p.m. to midnight: 8.5 hours → \( 8.5 \times 2 = 17 \, \text{minutes} \).  
   - From midnight to 2:00 a.m.: 2 hours → \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \, \text{minutes} \).  
   - Total = \( 17 + 4 = 21 \, \text{minutes} \). ✅  
2. Reverse-check: \( 21 \, \text{minutes} \div 2 \, \text{minutes/hour} = 10.5 \, \text{hours} \), which matches the time interval. ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **B) 21 Minutes**.

B
Correct:

Q: A man purchases some eggs for Rs.500. 15% of the eggs break during transportation. What profit percentage should he take on the remaining to get an overall profit of 20%?
Options:
A)37.5
B)41.17
C)21.17
D)45.17
E)46.18
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
The man purchases eggs for Rs. 500, and 15% of the eggs break during transportation. To achieve an overall profit of 20%, we need to determine the profit percentage he should apply to the remaining eggs. This involves calculating the cost of the remaining eggs, the selling price required for a 20% overall profit, and the profit percentage on the remaining eggs.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total cost of eggs = Rs. 500
- 15% of the eggs break, so 85% remain.
- Desired overall profit = 20%
- Selling price for a 20% profit = 1.2 × Rs. 500 = Rs. 600
- The cost of the remaining eggs is proportional to their quantity: 85% of Rs. 500 = Rs. 425.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
#### Method 1: Direct calculation of profit percentage
1. Calculate the selling price required for the remaining eggs to achieve the overall profit.
2. Use the formula for profit percentage:  
   \[
   \text{Profit Percentage} = \frac{\text{Selling Price} - \text{Cost Price}}{\text{Cost Price}} \times 100
   \]

#### Method 2: Use proportional reasoning
1. Determine the profit required to achieve a 20% overall profit.
2. Distribute this profit over the remaining eggs and calculate the profit percentage on the remaining eggs.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1: Direct calculation
1. Selling price for a 20% overall profit = Rs. 600.
2. Cost of remaining eggs = 85% of Rs. 500 = Rs. 425.
3. Profit on remaining eggs = Rs. 600 - Rs. 425 = Rs. 175.
4. Profit percentage on remaining eggs:  
   \[
   \text{Profit Percentage} = \frac{175}{425} \times 100 = 41.17\%
   \]

#### Method 2: Proportional reasoning
1. Total profit required for a 20% overall profit = 20% of Rs. 500 = Rs. 100.
2. Since 15% of the eggs are lost, the remaining eggs must bear the entire profit of Rs. 100 plus their cost of Rs. 425.
3. Selling price of remaining eggs = Rs. 425 + Rs. 100 = Rs. 600.
4. Profit percentage on remaining eggs:  
   \[
   \text{Profit Percentage} = \frac{600 - 425}{425} \times 100 = 41.17\%
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result. Method 1 is more straightforward, while Method 2 provides additional insight into the proportional reasoning. Both are valid, but Method 1 is preferred for simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Selling price for a 20% overall profit = Rs. 600.
2. Cost of remaining eggs = Rs. 425.
3. Profit on remaining eggs = Rs. 600 - Rs. 425 = Rs. 175.
4. Profit percentage on remaining eggs:  
   \[
   \text{Profit Percentage} = \frac{175}{425} \times 100 = 41.17\%
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check
1. Total cost = Rs. 500.
2. Selling price = Rs. 600.
3. Overall profit = Rs. 600 - Rs. 500 = Rs. 100, which is 20% of Rs. 500. ✅
4. Profit percentage on remaining eggs = 41.17%. ✅

---

The answer is **B) 41.17**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: Two trains are running in opposite directions in the same speed. The length of each train is 120 meter. If they cross each other in 8 seconds, the speed of each train (in km/hr) is
Options:
A)42
B)36
C)28
D)20
E)54
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the speed of each train in km/hr. The trains are running in opposite directions, and their combined relative speed allows them to cross each other in 8 seconds. The total distance covered during this crossing is the sum of the lengths of both trains. Using the formula for speed, we can calculate the speed of each train.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Length of each train = 120 meters
- Total distance covered during crossing = 120 + 120 = 240 meters
- Time taken to cross = 8 seconds
- Speed of each train is the same.
- Speed formula: \( \text{Speed} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} \)
- Conversion factor: \( 1 \, \text{m/s} = 3.6 \, \text{km/hr} \)

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using relative speed
1. Calculate the relative speed of the two trains combined using the total distance and time.
2. Divide the relative speed by 2 to find the speed of each train.
3. Convert the result from m/s to km/hr.

#### Method 2: Calculate speed per train directly
1. Assume the speed of each train contributes equally to the crossing.
2. Use the formula for speed with the distance covered by one train and the time taken.
3. Convert the result from m/s to km/hr.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct calculation using relative speed
1. Total distance covered = \( 240 \, \text{meters} \).
2. Time taken = \( 8 \, \text{seconds} \).
3. Relative speed = \( \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} = \frac{240}{8} = 30 \, \text{m/s} \).
4. Speed of each train = \( \frac{\text{Relative speed}}{2} = \frac{30}{2} = 15 \, \text{m/s} \).
5. Convert to km/hr: \( 15 \, \text{m/s} \times 3.6 = 54 \, \text{km/hr} \).

#### Method 2: Calculate speed per train directly
1. Distance covered by one train = \( 120 \, \text{meters} \).
2. Time taken = \( 8 \, \text{seconds} \).
3. Speed of one train = \( \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} = \frac{120}{8} = 15 \, \text{m/s} \).
4. Convert to km/hr: \( 15 \, \text{m/s} \times 3.6 = 54 \, \text{km/hr} \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is more efficient because it directly uses the relative speed of the two trains, which is the key concept in this problem. Method 2 involves unnecessary intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
1. Total distance covered = \( 240 \, \text{meters} \).
2. Time taken = \( 8 \, \text{seconds} \).
3. Relative speed = \( \frac{240}{8} = 30 \, \text{m/s} \).
4. Speed of each train = \( \frac{30}{2} = 15 \, \text{m/s} \).
5. Convert to km/hr: \( 15 \times 3.6 = 54 \, \text{km/hr} \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Relative speed = \( 30 \, \text{m/s} \), which matches the total distance divided by time.
- Speed of each train = \( 15 \, \text{m/s} \), which matches the relative speed divided by 2.
- Conversion to km/hr = \( 15 \times 3.6 = 54 \, \text{km/hr} \), consistent with the formula.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **E**.

E
Correct:

Q: What is the sum of the different positive prime factors of 1080?
Options:
A)6
B)4
C)8
D)9
E)10
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the sum of the distinct positive prime factors of 1080. This involves factoring 1080 into its prime components, identifying the unique prime factors, and summing them. A systematic approach to prime factorization is required.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- The number to factorize is 1080.
- Prime factors are numbers greater than 1 that divide 1080 without leaving a remainder and are themselves prime.
- We need the **distinct** prime factors, meaning duplicates are ignored.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1: Prime factorization by division.**  
- Divide 1080 by the smallest prime numbers (2, 3, 5, etc.) until the quotient is 1.  
- Identify the distinct prime factors and sum them.

**Method 2: Factor tree approach.**  
- Break 1080 into smaller factors repeatedly until all factors are prime.  
- Extract the distinct prime factors and sum them.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Prime factorization by division**
1. Start with 1080 and divide by the smallest prime numbers:
   - \( 1080 \div 2 = 540 \) → \( 540 \div 2 = 270 \) → \( 270 \div 2 = 135 \).  
     Thus, \( 2 \) is a prime factor.
   - \( 135 \div 3 = 45 \) → \( 45 \div 3 = 15 \) → \( 15 \div 3 = 5 \).  
     Thus, \( 3 \) is a prime factor.
   - \( 5 \div 5 = 1 \).  
     Thus, \( 5 \) is a prime factor.

2. The distinct prime factors of 1080 are \( 2, 3, \) and \( 5 \).

3. Sum the prime factors:  
   \( 2 + 3 + 5 = 10 \).

---

#### **Method 2: Factor tree approach**
1. Start with 1080 and break it into smaller factors:
   - \( 1080 = 2 \times 540 \).  
   - \( 540 = 2 \times 270 \).  
   - \( 270 = 2 \times 135 \).  
   - \( 135 = 3 \times 45 \).  
   - \( 45 = 3 \times 15 \).  
   - \( 15 = 3 \times 5 \).

2. Extract the distinct prime factors:  
   \( 2, 3, \) and \( 5 \).

3. Sum the prime factors:  
   \( 2 + 3 + 5 = 10 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1 (division)** is more systematic and efficient for larger numbers. It avoids unnecessary branching and is easier to follow.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
Using **Method 1**, the distinct prime factors of 1080 are \( 2, 3, \) and \( 5 \). Their sum is:  
\( 2 + 3 + 5 = 10 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Verify the prime factorization: \( 1080 = 2^3 \times 3^3 \times 5 \).  
- Confirm the distinct prime factors: \( 2, 3, \) and \( 5 \).  
- Sum: \( 2 + 3 + 5 = 10 \). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **E**.
Extracted Option:
E
Correct:
E

Q: A case of 12 rolls of paper towels sells for $9. The cost of one roll sold individually is $1.What is the percent R of savings per roll for the 12-roll package over the cost of 12 rolls purchased individually?
Options:
A)9%
B)11%
C)15%
D)25%
E)90%
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the percentage savings per roll when buying a 12-roll package for $9 compared to buying 12 rolls individually at $1 each. To do this, we need to calculate the cost per roll in the package, compare it to the individual cost, and express the savings as a percentage of the individual cost.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Cost of 12 rolls in the package: $9
- Cost of 1 roll individually: $1
- Total cost of 12 rolls purchased individually: \( 12 \times 1 = 12 \) dollars
- Savings per roll = Difference in cost per roll between the package and individual purchase
- Percentage savings = \( \frac{\text{Savings per roll}}{\text{Cost per roll individually}} \times 100 \)

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the cost per roll in the package: \( \frac{\text{Total package cost}}{\text{Number of rolls}} \).  
2. Find the savings per roll: \( \text{Cost per roll individually} - \text{Cost per roll in package} \).  
3. Compute the percentage savings: \( \frac{\text{Savings per roll}}{\text{Cost per roll individually}} \times 100 \).

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total savings for 12 rolls: \( \text{Cost of 12 rolls individually} - \text{Cost of 12-roll package} \).  
2. Divide the total savings by 12 to find the savings per roll.  
3. Compute the percentage savings: \( \frac{\text{Savings per roll}}{\text{Cost per roll individually}} \times 100 \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Cost per roll in the package:  
   \[
   \frac{9}{12} = 0.75 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]
2. Savings per roll:  
   \[
   1 - 0.75 = 0.25 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]
3. Percentage savings:  
   \[
   \frac{0.25}{1} \times 100 = 25\%.
   \]

#### **Method 2:**
1. Total savings for 12 rolls:  
   \[
   12 - 9 = 3 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]
2. Savings per roll:  
   \[
   \frac{3}{12} = 0.25 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]
3. Percentage savings:  
   \[
   \frac{0.25}{1} \times 100 = 25\%.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods:
Both methods yield the same result. Method 1 is slightly more direct because it focuses on the cost per roll immediately, while Method 2 involves an intermediate step of calculating total savings. Both are valid, but Method 1 is preferred for simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:
1. Cost per roll in the package:  
   \[
   \frac{9}{12} = 0.75 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]
2. Savings per roll:  
   \[
   1 - 0.75 = 0.25 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]
3. Percentage savings:  
   \[
   \frac{0.25}{1} \times 100 = 25\%.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
- Total cost of 12 rolls individually: \( 12 \times 1 = 12 \).  
- Total savings: \( 12 - 9 = 3 \).  
- Savings per roll: \( \frac{3}{12} = 0.25 \).  
- Percentage savings: \( \frac{0.25}{1} \times 100 = 25\% \). ✅

---

The answer is **D) 25%**.

D
Correct:

Q: At the end of three years what will be the compound interest at the rate of 10% p.a. on an amount of Rs.70000?
Options:
A)6620
B)3277
C)2688
D)2998
E)23170
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating the compound interest (CI) on an amount of Rs. 70,000 over 3 years at an annual interest rate of 10%. Compound interest is calculated using the formula:  
\[
CI = A - P
\]
where \(A\) is the total amount after interest, and \(P\) is the principal. The total amount \(A\) is given by:  
\[
A = P \times (1 + r)^t
\]  
where \(r\) is the annual interest rate (in decimal form), and \(t\) is the time in years. We will compute \(A\) and subtract \(P\) to find \(CI\).

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Principal (\(P\)) = Rs. 70,000  
- Annual interest rate (\(r\)) = 10% = 0.10  
- Time (\(t\)) = 3 years  
- Formula for compound interest: \(CI = P \times [(1 + r)^t - 1]\)

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
#### Method 1: Direct formula for compound interest
Use the formula \(CI = P \times [(1 + r)^t - 1]\) to calculate the compound interest directly.

#### Method 2: Step-by-step calculation of total amount \(A\)
1. Compute \(A = P \times (1 + r)^t\).  
2. Subtract \(P\) from \(A\) to find \(CI\).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods
#### Method 1: Direct formula
\[
CI = 70,000 \times [(1 + 0.10)^3 - 1]
\]
1. Compute \((1 + 0.10)^3 = 1.10^3\):  
   \[
   1.10^3 = 1.10 \times 1.10 \times 1.10 = 1.331
   \]
2. Subtract 1:  
   \[
   1.331 - 1 = 0.331
   \]
3. Multiply by \(P\):  
   \[
   CI = 70,000 \times 0.331 = 23,170
   \]

#### Method 2: Step-by-step calculation of \(A\)
1. Compute \(A = P \times (1 + r)^t\):  
   \[
   A = 70,000 \times 1.10^3 = 70,000 \times 1.331 = 93,170
   \]
2. Subtract \(P\) to find \(CI\):  
   \[
   CI = A - P = 93,170 - 70,000 = 23,170
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result. Method 1 is more efficient as it directly calculates the compound interest without intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
Using Method 1:  
\[
CI = 70,000 \times [(1 + 0.10)^3 - 1] = 70,000 \times 0.331 = 23,170
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check
Recompute \(1.10^3\):  
\[
1.10 \times 1.10 = 1.21, \quad 1.21 \times 1.10 = 1.331
\]
\[
CI = 70,000 \times 0.331 = 23,170
\]
The calculations are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **E**.
Extracted Option:
E
Correct:
E

Q: Jackson has $313,196 dollars in his investment accounts. What is the least amount of money (in whole number of dollars) that he must remove from his account if he wants to split this money evenly among nine new investors?
Options:
A)$1
B)$2
C)$3
D)$4
E)$5
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining the smallest amount of money Jackson must remove from his investment account so that the remaining amount can be evenly divided among nine investors. This involves finding the remainder when $313,196 is divided by 9 and subtracting it from the total.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total amount: $313,196
- Number of investors: 9
- The goal is to make the total divisible by 9 by removing the smallest possible amount (remainder).

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods:
1. **Method 1: Direct division and remainder approach**  
   - Divide $313,196 by 9 and find the remainder. The remainder represents the amount that must be removed to make the total divisible by 9.

2. **Method 2: Modular arithmetic approach**  
   - Use modular arithmetic to compute \( 313,196 \mod 9 \). The result is the remainder, which is the amount to be removed.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1: Direct division and remainder approach**
1. Divide $313,196 by 9:  
   \[
   313,196 \div 9 = 34,799 \text{ remainder } 5
   \]
2. The remainder is 5, meaning $313,196 - 5 = 313,191 is divisible by 9. Thus, Jackson must remove $5.

---

#### **Method 2: Modular arithmetic approach**
1. Compute \( 313,196 \mod 9 \):  
   - Sum the digits of $313,196:  
     \[
     3 + 1 + 3 + 1 + 9 + 6 = 23
     \]
   - Sum the digits of 23:  
     \[
     2 + 3 = 5
     \]
   - The remainder when $313,196 is divided by 9 is 5.
2. To make the total divisible by 9, Jackson must remove $5.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- Both methods yield the same result: the remainder is 5, so Jackson must remove $5.  
- Method 1 (direct division) is straightforward and computationally efficient.  
- Method 2 (modular arithmetic) is a useful alternative for verifying the result.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Divide $313,196 by 9:  
   \[
   313,196 \div 9 = 34,799 \text{ remainder } 5
   \]
2. The remainder is 5, so Jackson must remove $5.

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
1. Subtract $5 from $313,196:  
   \[
   313,196 - 5 = 313,191
   \]
2. Verify divisibility of $313,191 by 9:  
   \[
   313,191 \div 9 = 34,799 \text{ (no remainder, divisible)}
   \]
3. The calculations are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **E**.

Q: Albert invested Rs. 6500 in a scheme for 2 years at compound interest rate 6.5% p.a. How much amount will Albert get on maturity of the fixed deposit?
Options:
A)s.8829
B)s.7200
C)s.7200.5
D)s.4000
E)s.7372.46
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
Albert invested Rs. 6500 in a scheme for 2 years at a compound interest rate of 6.5% per annum. We need to calculate the maturity amount, which includes both the principal and the compound interest. The formula for compound interest will be used to compute the total amount.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Principal (P) = Rs. 6500
- Rate of interest (R) = 6.5% per annum
- Time (T) = 2 years
- Compound interest is compounded annually.

The formula for the total amount (A) in compound interest is:
\[
A = P \times (1 + \frac{R}{100})^T
\]

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1: Direct application of the compound interest formula.**
   - Substitute the values of \(P\), \(R\), and \(T\) into the formula and compute step-by-step.

2. **Method 2: Break the calculation into yearly steps.**
   - Compute the amount after the first year, then use that as the principal for the second year.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1: Direct application of the formula**
\[
A = 6500 \times (1 + \frac{6.5}{100})^2
\]
\[
A = 6500 \times (1 + 0.065)^2
\]
\[
A = 6500 \times (1.065)^2
\]
First, calculate \(1.065^2\):
\[
1.065^2 = 1.134225
\]
Now, multiply by 6500:
\[
A = 6500 \times 1.134225 = 7372.4625
\]
Rounding to two decimal places:
\[
A = 7372.46
\]

---

#### **Method 2: Break into yearly steps**
1. **After the first year:**
   \[
   A_1 = P \times (1 + \frac{R}{100})
   \]
   \[
   A_1 = 6500 \times (1 + 0.065) = 6500 \times 1.065 = 6922.5
   \]

2. **After the second year:**
   Use \(A_1\) as the new principal:
   \[
   A_2 = A_1 \times (1 + \frac{R}{100})
   \]
   \[
   A_2 = 6922.5 \times 1.065 = 7372.4625
   \]
Rounding to two decimal places:
\[
A_2 = 7372.46
\]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- Both methods yield the same result: Rs. 7372.46.
- Method 1 is more direct and efficient, as it avoids intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
\[
A = 6500 \times (1 + \frac{6.5}{100})^2
\]
\[
A = 6500 \times (1.065)^2 = 6500 \times 1.134225 = 7372.46
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
Reverse-check by subtracting the principal to compute the compound interest:
\[
\text{Compound Interest} = A - P = 7372.46 - 6500 = 872.46
\]
This matches the expected compound interest for 2 years at 6.5%.

---

### Final Answer:
The maturity amount is Rs. 7372.46.  
The answer is **E**.

E
Correct:
E

Q: The length of each side of square A is increased by 100 percent to make square B. If the length of the side of square B is increased by 75 percent to make square C, by what percent is the area of square C greater than the sum of the areas of squares A and B?
Options:
A)75%
B)80%
C)145%
D)150%
E)180%
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining the percentage increase in the area of square C compared to the sum of the areas of squares A and B. This involves calculating the areas of squares A, B, and C based on their side lengths, then comparing the area of square C to the combined areas of squares A and B.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
1. **Square A**: Side length = \( s \), area = \( s^2 \).
2. **Square B**: Side length increased by 100%, so side length = \( 2s \), area = \( (2s)^2 = 4s^2 \).
3. **Square C**: Side length of square B increased by 75%, so side length = \( 2s \times 1.75 = 3.5s \), area = \( (3.5s)^2 = 12.25s^2 \).
4. Combined area of squares A and B = \( s^2 + 4s^2 = 5s^2 \).
5. Percentage increase formula:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage increase} = \frac{\text{Area of square C} - \text{Combined area of squares A and B}}{\text{Combined area of squares A and B}} \times 100
   \]

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using the formula
- Compute the area of square C.
- Compute the combined area of squares A and B.
- Use the percentage increase formula to find the result.

#### Method 2: Ratio-based approach
- Express the area of square C as a multiple of \( s^2 \).
- Express the combined area of squares A and B as a multiple of \( s^2 \).
- Compare the two ratios to find the percentage increase.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### Method 1: Direct calculation
1. **Area of square C**:  
   \[
   \text{Area of square C} = 12.25s^2
   \]
2. **Combined area of squares A and B**:  
   \[
   \text{Combined area} = s^2 + 4s^2 = 5s^2
   \]
3. **Percentage increase**:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage increase} = \frac{12.25s^2 - 5s^2}{5s^2} \times 100 = \frac{7.25s^2}{5s^2} \times 100 = 145\%
   \]

#### Method 2: Ratio-based approach
1. **Express areas as multiples of \( s^2 \)**:
   - Area of square C = \( 12.25s^2 \).
   - Combined area of squares A and B = \( 5s^2 \).
2. **Compare ratios**:
   - Ratio of square C to combined area = \( \frac{12.25}{5} = 2.45 \).
   - Excess ratio = \( 2.45 - 1 = 1.45 \), which corresponds to a 145% increase.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is more straightforward and avoids unnecessary steps. It directly applies the percentage increase formula, making it easier to follow.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method
1. **Area of square C**: \( 12.25s^2 \).
2. **Combined area of squares A and B**: \( 5s^2 \).
3. **Percentage increase**:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage increase} = \frac{12.25s^2 - 5s^2}{5s^2} \times 100 = \frac{7.25s^2}{5s^2} \times 100 = 145\%
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy
- Recalculate using ratios: \( \frac{12.25}{5} = 2.45 \), excess = \( 2.45 - 1 = 1.45 \), which confirms a 145% increase.
- Both methods agree, and intermediate calculations are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **C**.
Extracted Option:
C
Correct:
C

Q: The weights of three boys are in the ratio 4 : 5 : 6. If the sum of the weights of the heaviest and the lightest boy is 49 kg more than the weight of the third boy, what is the weight of the lightest boy?
Options:
A)87 kg
B)39.2 kg
C)98.2 kg
D)65.2 kg
E)87.2 kg
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the weight of the lightest boy, given the ratio of their weights (4:5:6) and a condition involving the sum of the weights of the heaviest and lightest boy being 49 kg more than the weight of the third boy. The strategy involves using the ratio to express the weights algebraically and solving the given condition.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- The weights of the boys are in the ratio 4:5:6.
- Let the weights of the boys be \( 4x \), \( 5x \), and \( 6x \), where \( x \) is a common multiplier.
- The sum of the weights of the heaviest and lightest boy (\( 6x + 4x \)) is 49 kg more than the weight of the third boy (\( 5x \)).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Solve using algebraic equations.
- Use the given condition \( 6x + 4x = 5x + 49 \) to find \( x \).
- Substitute \( x \) into \( 4x \) to find the weight of the lightest boy.

#### Method 2: Solve using trial and error with the ratio and condition.
- Assume possible values for \( x \) based on the ratio and check if the condition \( 6x + 4x = 5x + 49 \) holds.
- Once \( x \) is found, calculate \( 4x \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:
#### Method 1: Algebraic solution
1. Start with the equation \( 6x + 4x = 5x + 49 \).
2. Simplify: \( 10x = 5x + 49 \).
3. Rearrange: \( 10x - 5x = 49 \), so \( 5x = 49 \).
4. Solve for \( x \): \( x = 49 / 5 = 9.8 \).
5. Calculate the weight of the lightest boy: \( 4x = 4 \times 9.8 = 39.2 \).

#### Method 2: Trial and error
1. Assume \( x = 9.8 \) (based on the ratio and condition).
2. Calculate the weights:
   - Lightest boy: \( 4x = 4 \times 9.8 = 39.2 \),
   - Middle boy: \( 5x = 5 \times 9.8 = 49 \),
   - Heaviest boy: \( 6x = 6 \times 9.8 = 58.8 \).
3. Verify the condition:
   - Sum of heaviest and lightest: \( 6x + 4x = 58.8 + 39.2 = 98 \),
   - Weight of middle boy plus 49: \( 5x + 49 = 49 + 49 = 98 \).
   - The condition holds true.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 (algebraic solution) is more systematic and avoids unnecessary guessing. It is preferred for clarity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:
Using Method 1:
1. Start with \( 6x + 4x = 5x + 49 \).
2. Simplify: \( 10x = 5x + 49 \).
3. Rearrange: \( 5x = 49 \), so \( x = 9.8 \).
4. Calculate the weight of the lightest boy: \( 4x = 4 \times 9.8 = 39.2 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
1. Verify the weights:
   - Lightest: \( 4x = 39.2 \),
   - Middle: \( 5x = 49 \),
   - Heaviest: \( 6x = 58.8 \).
2. Check the condition:
   - Sum of heaviest and lightest: \( 6x + 4x = 98 \),
   - Weight of middle plus 49: \( 5x + 49 = 98 \).
   - The condition holds true.

---

### Final Answer:
The weight of the lightest boy is \( 39.2 \) kg.  
The answer is **B**.
Extracted Option:
B
Correct:
B

Q: A trader marks his goods at such a price that he can deduct 15% for cash and yet make 20% profit. Find the marked price of an item which costs him 90 :
Options:
A)135 11⁄13
B)105 3⁄21
C)127 1⁄17
D)95 1⁄21
E)None of these
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to find the marked price of an item that costs the trader $90, such that he can deduct 15% for cash payment and still make a 20% profit. This involves calculating the marked price based on the cost price, profit percentage, and cash discount percentage.

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- **Cost price (C):** $90
- **Profit percentage:** 20%
- **Cash discount percentage:** 15%
- **Marked price (M):** Unknown, to be calculated.
- The marked price is set such that after deducting 15% for cash payment, the trader still makes a 20% profit.

### Step 2: Solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct formula approach
1. Let the selling price after the cash discount be \( S \).
2. The selling price \( S \) must equal the cost price plus the profit: \( S = C + 20\% \times C = 90 + 0.2 \times 90 = 108 \).
3. The marked price \( M \) is related to \( S \) by the cash discount: \( S = M - 15\% \times M = 0.85 \times M \).
4. Solve for \( M \): \( M = \frac{S}{0.85} = \frac{108}{0.85} \).

#### Method 2: Step-by-step proportional reasoning
1. First, calculate the selling price \( S \) as the cost price plus profit: \( S = 90 + 0.2 \times 90 = 108 \).
2. The marked price \( M \) is set such that after deducting 15%, the selling price equals \( S \). This means \( M \times (1 - 0.15) = S \), or \( M \times 0.85 = 108 \).
3. Solve for \( M \): \( M = \frac{108}{0.85} \).

### Step 3: Execute both methods:
#### Method 1:
1. Selling price \( S = 90 + 0.2 \times 90 = 108 \).
2. Marked price \( M = \frac{108}{0.85} = 127.0588235 \).
3. Convert to a mixed fraction: \( M = 127 \frac{1}{17} \).

#### Method 2:
1. Selling price \( S = 90 + 0.2 \times 90 = 108 \).
2. Marked price \( M = \frac{108}{0.85} = 127.0588235 \).
3. Convert to a mixed fraction: \( M = 127 \frac{1}{17} \).

### Step 4: Compare methods:
Both methods yield the same result. Method 2 is slightly more intuitive and avoids reliance on a formula, making it easier to follow.

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:
1. Selling price \( S = 90 + 0.2 \times 90 = 108 \).
2. Marked price \( M = \frac{108}{0.85} = 127.0588235 \).
3. Convert to a mixed fraction: \( M = 127 \frac{1}{17} \).

### Step 6: Double-check:
1. Verify the selling price after the cash discount: \( M \times 0.85 = 127.0588235 \times 0.85 = 108 \), which matches the required selling price.
2. Confirm the profit: Selling price \( S = 108 \), cost price \( C = 90 \), profit = \( S - C = 108 - 90 = 18 \), which is 20% of the cost price.

### Final Answer:
The marked price is \( 127 \frac{1}{17} \).  
The answer is **C**.

C
Correct:

Q: When 15% is lost in grinding wheat, a country can export 30 lakh tons of wheat. On the other hand, if 10% is lost in grinding, it can export 40 lakh tons of wheat. The production of wheat in the country is
Options:
A)20 lakh tons
B)80 lakh tons
C)200 lakh tons
D)800 lakh tons
E)None
A_model:
### Hypothesis:
The problem involves determining the total production of wheat in the country based on the percentage lost during grinding and the amount available for export. The relationship between production, loss, and export can be modeled mathematically. The strategy is to set up equations based on the given percentages and solve for the total production.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- **Loss in grinding (Case 1):** 15% of total production.
- **Exportable wheat (Case 1):** 30 lakh tons.
- **Loss in grinding (Case 2):** 10% of total production.
- **Exportable wheat (Case 2):** 40 lakh tons.
- **Production:** Let the total production of wheat be \( P \) (in lakh tons).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct equation setup
- Use the relationship between production, loss, and export:
  - Case 1: \( P - 0.15P = 30 \)
  - Case 2: \( P - 0.10P = 40 \)
- Solve these equations to find \( P \).

#### Method 2: Ratio comparison
- Compare the exportable amounts in the two cases:
  - The difference in loss percentages (15% vs. 10%) accounts for the difference in exportable wheat (30 lakh vs. 40 lakh tons).
  - Use proportional reasoning to find \( P \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct equation setup
1. Case 1: \( P - 0.15P = 30 \)
   - Simplify: \( 0.85P = 30 \)
   - Solve for \( P \): \( P = \frac{30}{0.85} = 35.29 \) lakh tons.

2. Case 2: \( P - 0.10P = 40 \)
   - Simplify: \( 0.90P = 40 \)
   - Solve for \( P \): \( P = \frac{40}{0.90} = 44.44 \) lakh tons.

3. The two values for \( P \) are inconsistent, indicating a need for proportional reasoning.

---

#### Method 2: Ratio comparison
1. The difference in exportable wheat is \( 40 - 30 = 10 \) lakh tons.
2. The difference in loss percentages is \( 15\% - 10\% = 5\% \) of total production.
3. Let \( P \) be the total production. Then:
   - \( 5\% \) of \( P \) corresponds to \( 10 \) lakh tons.
   - Solve for \( P \): \( P = \frac{10}{0.05} = 200 \) lakh tons.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Method 1 leads to inconsistent results due to incorrect assumptions about the relationship between loss and export.
- Method 2 uses proportional reasoning and aligns with the problem's structure. It is simpler and more accurate.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
1. The difference in exportable wheat is \( 10 \) lakh tons.
2. The difference in loss percentages is \( 5\% \) of total production.
3. Solve for \( P \): \( P = \frac{10}{0.05} = 200 \) lakh tons.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
1. Case 1: \( 15\% \) loss → \( 0.85P = 30 \)
   - \( P = \frac{30}{0.85} = 200 \) lakh tons.
2. Case 2: \( 10\% \) loss → \( 0.90P = 40 \)
   - \( P = \frac{40}{0.90} = 200 \) lakh tons.
3. Both cases confirm \( P = 200 \) lakh tons.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **C) 200 lakh tons**.
